S069\n  name: Intrusive_Symptoms\n  description: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: A clank from a cart or the smell of hot oil can put me right back in that bay, seeing my coworker by the machine like it’s happening in front of me all over again.\n- key: S070\n  name: Trauma_Avoidance\n  description: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: I go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay, I won’t talk about the accident if I can help it, and I quit going to the industrial-history talks I used to enjoy because the old machinery pictures set me off.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I remember the shift and I remember people yelling afterward, but there’s a whole stretch between the first alarm and seeing him down that is just blank no matter how hard I try to piece it together.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I don’t trust that plant or the people running it anymore, and some days I feel like I’m damaged goods who can’t even do the job I spent my life getting good at.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks or stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I’m here because I keep snapping at people, like at that safety meeting when I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the Even in the break room or at rehearsal, I’m watching doors, listening for sudden machine noises, and checking who’s moving behind me because my body won’t believe I’m safe.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve missed so many shifts and lost so much patience with trainees that I’m no longer trusted to mentor the younger inspectors, and I’ve pulled back from community theater because I can’t reliably get through rehearsals.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S077\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like snapping at people has become a habitual way for you to deal with stress or frustration?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. When I get stressed or frustrated, I go straight to snapping before I even think. That’s why I’m here. Like at that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve something specific when you snap at people, or is it more of an automatic reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s more automatic. I’m not trying to get something out of it, really. I just get worked up and I’m already snapping before I can stop myself. I mean, I get stressed, but I don’t have some specific thing I feel like I have to do to make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider yourself a perfectionist?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call myself a perfectionist. I like things done right, especially at work, but I don’t think I’m obsessive about everything being perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel are hard to change?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve got routines like anybody does, but nothing I feel stuck doing or that I can’t change if I have to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you snap at people?\n\nPatient: No. Not when it happens. It’s like I’m already yelling before I’ve decided to. That’s part of why I’m here—I keep snapping at people. At a safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to think before you react when you snap at people?\n\nPatient: Not really. By the time I realize what I’m doing, I’m already snapping. Like that safety meeting—I was yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act like they’re afraid to even ask me things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yes. I get overwhelmed, especially when I’m angry. It comes on fast, and I snap before I can get a handle on it. That safety meeting is a good example—I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like snapping at people has become a habitual way for you to deal with stress or frustration?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. When I get stressed or frustrated, I go straight to snapping before I even think. That’s why I’m here. Like at that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve something specific when you snap at people, or is it more of an automatic reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s more automatic. I’m not trying to get something out of it, really. I just get worked up and I’m already snapping before I can stop myself. That’s what happened in that safety meeting—I started yelling, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to even ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like snapping at people helps you feel more in control of a situation?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a second, yeah. When I snap, it feels like I’m taking control because I’m loud and people stop. But honestly, it’s not really control. I’m here because I keep doing that—like at the safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your snapping at people is part of a broader way you interact with others in general?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. It’s not just one bad moment. I keep snapping at people when I get worked up. Like I said, at that safety meeting I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to ask me anything. That bothers me, because I don’t want to be that kind of person.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've always had a tendency to snap at people, even before the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: No, not like this. I mean, I could get annoyed before, sure, but I wasn’t snapping at people all the time. Now I get worked up and it just comes out before I can stop it. That safety meeting was a good example—I started yelling, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to even ask me things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of snapping at people is something that's become a part of who you are, rather than just a reaction to specific situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s who I am, but it’s starting to feel like something I do too often now. When I get stressed or frustrated, I go straight to snapping before I even think. That’s why I’m here. Like at that safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve something specific when you snap at people, or is it more of an automatic reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s more automatic. I’m not trying to get something out of it, really. I just get worked up and I’m already snapping before I can stop myself. That’s what happened in that safety meeting—I started yelling, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to even ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like snapping at people helps you feel more in control of a situation?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a second, yeah. When I snap, it feels like I’m taking control because I’m loud and people stop. But honestly, it’s not really control. I’m here because I keep doing that—like at the safety meeting, I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your snapping at people is part of a broader way you interact with others in general?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. It’s not just one bad moment. I keep snapping at people when I get worked up. Like I said, at that safety meeting I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors act scared to ask me anything. That bothers me, because I don’t want to be that kind of person.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've always had a tendency to snap at people, even before the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: No, not like this. I mean, I could get annoyed before, sure, but I wasn’t snapping at people all the time. Now I get worked up and it just comes out before I can stop it. That safety meeting was a good example—I started yelling, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to even ask me things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of snapping at people is something that's become a part of who you are, rather than just a reaction to specific situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s who I am, but it’s starting to feel like something I do too often now. I’m here because I keep snapping at people. That safety meeting still bothers me—I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still snap at people if you changed jobs or were in a different social environment?\n\nPatient: I think it might still happen, yeah. It doesn’t feel like it’s just the job itself. I’m here because I keep snapping at people, and that safety meeting kind of showed it—I started yelling before I could stop myself. Now the younger inspectors act scared to ask me anything, and that really bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to snap at people is something that has been a part of your personality for most of your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been part of me my whole life. That bothers me, because I don’t want to be that kind of person.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've always had a tendency to snap at people, even before the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: No, not like this. That safety meeting was a good example—I started yelling, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to even ask me things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of snapping at people is something that's become a part of who you are, rather than just a reaction to specific situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s who I am, but it’s starting to feel like something I do too often now. I’m here because I keep snapping at people. That safety meeting still bothers me—I started yelling before I could stop myself, and now the younger inspectors seem scared to ask me anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still snap at people if you changed jobs or were in a different social environment?\n\nPatient: I think it might still happen, yeah.
